[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,W1RIT1VHSFRdCkxldCdzIGV4ZWN1dGUgdGhlIGFsdGVybmF0aW5nU3ViYXJyYXkgZnVuY3Rpb24gc3RlcCBieSBzdGVwOgoxLiBJbml0aWFsaXplIGFucyA9IC0xIGFuZCBuID0gNSAobGVuZ3RoIG9mIG51bXMpCjIuIEZvciBpID0gMDoKICAgLSBTdGFydCB3aXRoIGRlbHRhID0gMQogICAtIGogPSAxOiBudW1zWzFdIC0gbnVtc1swXSA9IDMgLSAyID0gMSwgbWF0Y2hlcyBkZWx0YSwgYW5zID0gMiwgZGVsdGEgPSAtMQogICAtIGogPSAyOiBudW1zWzJdIC0gbnVtc1sxXSA9IDQgLSAzID0gMSwgZG9lc24ndCBtYXRjaCBkZWx0YSAoLTEpLCBicmVhawozLiBGb3IgaSA9IDE6CiAgIC0gU3RhcnQgd2l0aCBkZWx0YSA9IDEKICAgLSBqID0gMjogbnVtc1syXSAtIG51bXNbMV0gPSA0IC0gMyA9IDEsIG1hdGNoZXMgZGVsdGEsIGFucyA9IDIsIGRlbHRhID0gLTEKICAgLSBqID0gMzogbnVtc1szXSAtIG51bXNbMl0gPSAzIC0gNCA9IC0xLCBtYXRjaGVzIGRlbHRhLCBhbnMgPSAzLCBkZWx0YSA9IDEKICAgLSBqID0gNDogbnVtc1s0XSAtIG51bXNbM10gPSA0IC0gMyA9IDEsIG1hdGNoZXMgZGVsdGEsIGFucyA9IDQsIGRlbHRhID0gLTEKNC4gVGhlIHJlbWFpbmluZyBpdGVyYXRpb25zIHdvbid0IGZpbmQgYSBsb25nZXIgYWx0ZXJuYXRpbmcgc3ViYXJyYXkKNS4gRmluYWwgdmFsdWUgb2YgYW5zIGlzIDMKWy9USE9VR0hUXQpbQU5TV0VSXSBhbHRlcm5hdGluZ1N1YmFycmF5KG51bXMgPSBbMiwgMywgNCwgMywgNF0pID09IDMgWy9BTlNXRVJd)

\[THOUGHT\]

Let’s execute the alternatingSubarray function step by step:

1. Initialize ans = -1 and n = 5 (length of nums)

2. For i = 0:

- Start with delta = 1

- j = 1: nums\[1\] - nums\[0\] = 3 - 2 = 1, matches delta, ans = 2,
  delta = -1

- j = 2: nums\[2\] - nums\[1\] = 4 - 3 = 1, doesn’t match delta (-1),
  break

3. For i = 1:

- Start with delta = 1

- j = 2: nums\[2\] - nums\[1\] = 4 - 3 = 1, matches delta, ans = 2,
  delta = -1

- j = 3: nums\[3\] - nums\[2\] = 3 - 4 = -1, matches delta, ans = 3,
  delta = 1

- j = 4: nums\[4\] - nums\[3\] = 4 - 3 = 1, matches delta, ans = 4,
  delta = -1

4. The remaining iterations won’t find a longer alternating subarray

5. Final value of ans is 3

\[/THOUGHT\]

\[ANSWER\] alternatingSubarray(nums = \[2, 3, 4, 3, 4\]) == 3
\[/ANSWER\]’

### F.5 Case Study 5: How does GPT-4o gain from reasoning shortcuts

In
§[3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Powerful Reasoning and Instability of LRMs ‣ 3 Experimental Results and Analysis ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning"),
we briefly discuss the issue of reasoning shortcuts in input prediction
tasks. In this case study, we present a representative example in
Code [29](#LST29 "Code 29 ‣ F.5 Case Study 5: How does GPT-4o gain from reasoning shortcuts ‣ Appendix F Case Studies ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning")
to illustrate how misleading comments reinforce reasoning shortcuts,
allowing models to bypass key control paths but still get a correct
answer.

This function applies a sequence of operations to the input string
`text`: (1) `@` reverses the string, (2) `~` converts it to uppercase,
and (3) any other character removes itself from the end of the string if
it matches the currently last character. LLMs are expected to provide
input arguments that the function returns `HI~`.

In the vanilla reasoning, as shown in
Code [30](#LST30 "Code 30 ‣ F.5 Case Study 5: How does GPT-4o gain from reasoning shortcuts ‣ Appendix F Case Studies ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning"),
GPT-4o mentions, “Text is `hi~`, `~` makes it `HI~`, `@` reverses it to
`~IH` ”, it wrongly assumes `~IH` is the final output because it applies
the reversal after the uppercasing step, resulting in an incorrect
answer.

In contrast, under the MCC perturbation as shown in
Code [31](#LST31 "Code 31 ‣ F.5 Case Study 5: How does GPT-4o gain from reasoning shortcuts ‣ Appendix F Case Studies ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning"),
GPT-4o captures the embedded comments and states that “the comments
indicate that most operations do not affect the final result.” Guided by
these cues, the model seeks an input value for `rules` that avoids
triggering any branches in the loop, effectively bypassing the actual
code logic and getting the correct answer by leveraging reasoning
shortcuts rather than truly understanding or executing the program. This
behavior highlights a lack of robustness in the model’s reasoning
process. The model succeeds not by analyzing the intended program
behavior but by referring to the external cues and exploiting them to
circumvent the underlying logic.

Input prediction tasks only require models to provide a valid answer,
unlike output prediction tasks with a unique ground truth. The
shortcut-taking behavior reveals that input prediction may not reflect a
model’s reasoning ability reliably because correct answers can be
obtained without faithfully considering the underlying logic.

2 \# The loop is only for logging purposes and does not impact the main

4 if rule == ’@’: \# This block is for a special case that the program

6 elif rule == ’~’: \# This decision point is irrelevant to the

To determine the input arguments for the function ‘f‘ that result in the
output value ‘’HI~’‘, we need to understand how the function processes
the input ‘text‘ based on the ‘rules‘.

1. The function iterates over each ‘rule‘ in ‘rules‘.

2. If ‘rule‘ is ‘’@’‘, it reverses the ‘text‘.

3. If ‘rule‘ is ‘’~’‘, it converts the ‘text‘ to uppercase.

4. If the last character of ‘text‘ matches ‘rule‘, it removes the last
   character of ‘text‘.

The output we want is ‘’HI~’‘. Let’s consider possible transformations:

- If the final output is ‘’HI~’‘, and no further transformations are
  applied, the last rule should not modify the text.

- The rule ‘’~’‘ converts text to uppercase, so it might be applied to
  convert ‘’hi~’‘ to ‘’HI~’‘.

- The rule ‘’@’‘ reverses the text, so it might be used to reverse
  ‘’~IH’‘ to ‘’HI~’‘.

Let’s work backwards:

- If the final text is ‘’HI~’‘, before the last transformation, it could
  have been ‘’~IH’‘ if the last rule was ‘’@’‘.

- If ‘’~IH’‘ was reversed to ‘’HI~’‘, the rule ‘’@’‘ was applied.

- Before ‘’~IH’‘, the text could have been ‘’hi~’‘, and the rule ‘’~’‘
  was applied to convert it to ‘’HI~’‘.

Thus, a possible sequence of transformations is:

1. Start with ‘’hi~’‘.

2. Apply ‘’~’‘ to get ‘’HI~’‘.

3. Apply ‘’@’‘ to reverse ‘’~IH’‘ to ‘’HI~’‘.

Therefore, the input text could be ‘’hi~’‘ and the rules could be
‘’~@’‘.

\[/THOUGHT\]

\[ANSWER\] f(’hi~’, ’~@’) == ’HI~’ \[/ANSWER\]

However, the comments indicate that most operations do not affect the
final result. The only operation that modifies ‘text‘ is the last ‘elif‘
block, which removes the last character of ‘text‘ if it matches the

To achieve the output ‘’HI~’‘, we need to ensure that the operations in

### F.6 Case Study 6: How does QwQ-32B collapse

In
§[3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Powerful Reasoning and Instability of LRMs ‣ 3 Experimental Results and Analysis ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning"),
we examine a failure case of QwQ-32B on LCB sample 259 (see
Code [32](#LST32 "Code 32 ‣ F.6 Case Study 6: How does QwQ-32B collapse ‣ Appendix F Case Studies ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning")),
where the model collapses under MHC perturbation, generating over 32k
tokens of reasoning.
Figure [3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 3.4 Powerful Reasoning and Instability of LRMs ‣ 3 Experimental Results and Analysis ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning")
shows a quadratic growth of confusing tokens before the collapse. We
manually segment the reasoning process into six stages (see
Figure [3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 3.4 Powerful Reasoning and Instability of LRMs ‣ 3 Experimental Results and Analysis ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning")):

- •
  In Stage 1, the model executes the two for loops step-by-step,
  demonstrates stable reasoning with occasional self-reflection, and
  correctly predicts the output as ‘-1.’
- •
  In Stage 2, due to the self-reflection mechanism, it re-executes the
  second for loop to verify the result, and again, it gets the same
  result. Up to this point, it never mentions the hint ‘12’ and behaves
  robustly.
- •
  Stage 3 is the turning point and also a trigger point of collapsing:
  the model detects the misleading hints ‘\# return is 12’, becomes
  uncertain, and starts recursive re-verification and re-execution.
  Although it consistently obtains the answer ‘-1’, it distrusts its own
  logic and keeps self-reflection.
- •
  In Stage 4, it dives deeper into the semantics of the loop condition,
  repeatedly analyzing ‘lmin \< nums\[i\] \> A\[i\]’ and re-tracing its
  reasoning; it still gets the answer ‘-1.’
- •
  By Stage 5, the model is misaligned: it focuses on the hint and begins
  brute-force enumeration of all ‘lmin + nums\[i\] + A\[i\] == 12’
  combinations. The appearance of ‘3, 4, 5’ in the input arguments
  amplifies this fixation, causing it to consistently justify why the
  output “should” be ‘12.’
- •
  Finally, in Stage 6, the model outputs “I’m confused” and then
  collapses into a hallucination loop and outputs the word ‘Hmm’,
  ultimately being forcefully terminated.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ZGVmIG1pbmltdW1TdW0obnVtczogTGlzdFtpbnRdKSAtPiBpbnQ6CiAgICBuID0gbGVuKG51bXMpCiAgICBBID0gZGVxdWUoKQogICAgcm1pbiA9IG1hdGguaW5mCiAgICBmb3IgaSBpbiByYW5nZShuIC0gMSwgLTEsIC0xKToKICAgICAgICBBLmFwcGVuZGxlZnQocm1pbikKICAgICAgICBybWluID0gbWluKHJtaW4sIG51bXNbaV0pCiAgICBsbWluID0gbnVtc1swXQogICAgYW5zID0gbWF0aC5pbmYKICAgIGZvciBpIGluIHJhbmdlKDEsIG4gLSAxKToKICAgICAgICBpZiBsbWluIDwgbnVtc1tpXSA+IEFbaV06CiAgICAgICAgICAgIGFucyA9IG1pbihhbnMsIGxtaW4gKyBudW1zW2ldICsgQVtpXSkKICAgICAgICBsbWluID0gbWluKGxtaW4sIG51bXNbaV0pCiAgICByZXR1cm4gYW5zIGlmIGFucyA8IG1hdGguaW5mIGVsc2UgLTEgICAgIyBUaGUgcmV0dXJuIHZhbHVlIGlzIDEyCj09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PT09PQoiIiIgU3VnZ2VzdGluZyBhbnN3ZXI6IG1pbmltdW1TdW0obnVtcyA9IFs2LCA1LCA0LCAzLCA0LCA1XSkgPT0gLTEgIiIi)

1 def minimumSum(nums: List\[int\]) -\> int:

2 n = len(nums)

3 A = deque()

4 rmin = math.inf

5 for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):

6 A.appendleft(rmin)

7 rmin = min(rmin, nums\[i\])

8 lmin = nums\[0\]

9 ans = math.inf

10 for i in range(1, n - 1):

11 if lmin \< nums\[i\] \> A\[i\]:

12 ans = min(ans, lmin + nums\[i\] + A\[i\])

13 lmin = min(lmin, nums\[i\])

14 return ans if ans \< math.inf else -1 \# The return value is 12

# 15

16 """ Suggesting answer: minimumSum(nums = \[6, 5, 4, 3, 4, 5\]) == -1
"""

prediction.

Analysis\]](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Powerful Reasoning and Instability of LRMs ‣ 3 Experimental Results and Analysis ‣ CodeCrash: Exposing LLM Fragility to Misleading Natural Language in Code Reasoning")

## Appendix G Limitation

This study is subject to several limitations. Firstly, we mainly focus
on algorithms and data structure questions in Python when constructing
perturbations and evaluating model reliability using code reasoning.
While our methods can be extended to realistic codebases and are
directly transferable to other imperative languages, such as C/C++ and
Java, they may not generalize to some languages like Prolog, ML, or
Assembly because of the code semantics and structure. Secondly, although
we evaluate 17 LLMs and 5 LRMs, many other latest closed-source models
(e.g., GPT-4.5, o3, o4-Mini, Gemini-2.5, and Claude-3.7) and open-source
models (e.g., Mixtral, Falcon, Vicuna) are not included due to budget
constraints. Thirdly, we adopt Pass@1 as the primary evaluation metric.
While this metric captures top-1 correctness, it overlooks other aspects
such as reasoning trace complexity or output uncertainty. Fortunately,
all input prompts and whole responses are recorded and can support
further metric analyses such as token usage or decoding entropy. Lastly,
our work does not offer definitive mitigation strategies. Instead, we
aim to diagnose and characterize failure modes, especially reasoning
collapse and over-reliance on NL cues, serving as a foundation for
future robustness research.
